NVIDIA's hardware, software ecosystem, interconnects and development tools form a set of competitive factors different from traditional oilfield services. It is not a drilling contractor paid simply by hours worked. Yet upstream suppliers remain exposed if downstream customers cut equipment budgets, change architectures or delay data-center commissioning. Selling essential equipment does not automatically remove order risk caused by customers' failure to recover cash.

Customer concentration must be read according to disclosure definitions: direct customers may be distributors or system manufacturers, while end users may be cloud providers or may purchase through dedicated compute operators. Direct-customer shares are not treated as end-customer shares, and no calculation is made of how much riskier NVIDIA is than an oilfield-service company before entities and periods are aligned.

The testing sequence is whether end-customer construction plans have changed, whether delivery and payment have changed, whether alternative architectures erode unit value, and whether suppliers continue supporting purchases through financing arrangements. The residual-value guarantees and purchase warrants discussed in this chapter show that upstream earnings and downstream credit can be connected beyond equipment sales. This is closer to the substance of risk-bearing than simply comparing who is the "shovel seller."

IV. Technological Efficiency, Prices and Replacement Speed: Where the Analogy Most Easily Breaks Down

Oil Volume and Price Are Not Token Volume and Price

Oil prices are an important common market signal, whereas AI-service pricing depends on the task, model quality, input/output mix, caching, batching, reasoning depth and latency requirements. The same one million tokens may not produce the same work outcome, so the cheapest listed price alone should not be used to judge whether the whole application market has become commoditized.

Improved model efficiency can lower unit costs and expand usage, and can also trigger competitive price cuts. The direction of total revenue and profit depends on their relative pace. Illustratively, if the price of a comparable task falls 15% and paid usage rises 20%, the revenue change is 0.85 × 1.20 − 1 = +2%; if usage rises only 10%, revenue changes by −6.5%. These are scenario calculations, not observed industry demand elasticities. Costs must also be deducted from revenue before discussing profit.

Open source and new models can provide evidence of competitive pressure, but the compute bill for a particular training run is not a company's entire cost of research, data, experimentation, personnel and operations. Nor can one benchmark result establish that substitution has been completed across all commercial tasks. Until usage, reliability and customer switching costs are validated, a technical demonstration cannot be converted directly into a revenue-loss rate.

Asset Replacement Is Not the Same Kind of "Decline"

Declining well output drives the need for continued drilling. AI accelerators, even while working normally, may become less economical because of changes in cost per task, memory, interconnects or software compatibility. Older equipment can move to different workloads; genuine obsolescence must be assessed through secondary uses, rental prices and electricity costs, rather than assuming mandatory retirement at a certain accounting age.

Land, grid connections, buildings, cooling systems and chips within the same AI project cannot share one residual-value curve. Data-center and power assets may serve several generations of equipment, or may require retrofits because of regional oversupply or an inability to support new power densities. A cash flow model must allow both "efficiency gains extend economic use" and "new architectures accelerate replacement," rather than retaining only the path that supports a bubble judgment.

Policy and National Strategy: Changing Constraints Without Guaranteeing Returns

Shale oil is also affected by land, water, emissions, pipelines and transportation; AI is constrained by electricity, data, exports and product compliance. Both have policy variables that may change project costs, start dates and addressable sales. Regulatory tail risk cannot be described as unique to AI.

National-strategy emphasis may change project conditions through research support, permits, energy construction, procurement or financing instruments, but does not mean the government backstops every company's entire investment. Research must distinguish government policy, private equity funding, corporate commitments and sovereign capital transaction by transaction. In particular, an investment amount cannot be booked as fiscal expenditure already incurred simply because a project carries a national-strategy label.

This also means that a cycle need not end at a single market-clearing moment: some projects may continue because of strategic demand, while others remain constrained by returns and debt maturities. Support can change scenario weights, but belongs in a quantitative model only once amounts, duration and conditions have been obtained.

V. Three Research Tasks Derived from the Historical Analogy

Verify Technological Success, Operating Success and Investment Success Separately

Technological success concerns task capability, adoption and productivity; operating success concerns paid retention, cash gross profit, return on capital and competitive position; investment success additionally considers entry valuation, financing costs, dilution and holding period. The three layers may materialize sequentially, or their benefits may accrue to different parties. Evidence for one layer cannot replace evidence for the other two.

Thus, "the technology is real" only refutes the claim that it is completely useless; it does not automatically refute overvaluation. "Some participants lose money" likewise does not prove failure of the entire technology's diffusion. To judge whether a view has been validated, state in advance which layer was predicted and what data will be used to score it.

Distinguish Three Paths of Stress Transmission

The demand path: paid retention or customer returns fall short of expectations, affecting new purchases and actual use; this may then transmit to operator revenue and orders for newly built equipment. Customer, contract or billable-usage data is needed; share prices alone cannot establish it.

The price-and-efficiency path: usage keeps rising, but prices for equivalent quality fall faster than costs improve, putting profit and equipment economic life under pressure. This path does not require demand to collapse and does not guarantee simultaneous damage to suppliers. Volume, price and unit cost must be examined at each layer.

The financing-and-delivery path: delays in electricity connection, construction, acceptance or lease commencement extend cash flow shortfalls; if refinancing tightens, some projects may be postponed or restructured. Long-term customer demand may still exist. Those first affected are participants with greater maturity mismatch and weaker financing buffers.

These three paths can reinforce one another, or be eased by retention growth, cost improvements, contractual protection or external funding. Separating them helps explain why strong orders, ambitious construction plans and credit pressure at individual companies can coexist in the same quarter.

Constrain the Analogy with Disconfirming Evidence

If end-customer cash benefits improve consistently, unsubsidized paid use expands, customers become less dependent on financing, and upstream suppliers continue receiving stable payments without new backstops, the judgment that the industry is "mainly financing-driven, with insufficient end-customer returns" should weaken.

If usage grows without corresponding improvement in the quality of paid demand, customer retention and cash profit, or if free cash flow improves mainly through sharp cuts in necessary reinvestment, one cannot lightly declare capital recovery complete. The shale-oil sample in which "investment contracted faster than cash flow" reminds us to separate the causes of cash improvement each time, rather than treating every move into positive cash flow as a recovery.

VI. Who Can Retain the Gains: Four Roles Still Require Transaction-by-Transaction Testing

"Low-cost operators" are companies that can generate cash sustainably at lower selling prices, not a permanent title for any recognized leader. Customer retention, delivery reliability, cash from existing businesses and cost per task can support this role, but the entry valuation may still exhaust all the operating advantage in advance. The most dangerous combination of grand narratives and growth targets is one in which capacity, tokens or ARR keep rising while nobody explains how much additional cash is needed to sustain growth.

"Consolidators" need deployable cash and the ability to identify assets that can be integrated into their own businesses. Cheap GPUs lacking electricity or customers, or requiring expensive migration, are not necessarily cheap operable compute. Low-price acquisitions may also come with leases, guarantees, environmental obligations and demolition liabilities. Cash provides options, not guaranteed acquisition returns.

"Providers of scarce infrastructure" may include wafer fabrication, equipment, electricity supply, grid access and cooling, but the value of charging rights depends on the contract: usage-based charges, minimum payments, take-or-pay arrangements, government-approved returns and customer credit are not the same risk. Stopping compute workloads may save some electricity without ending PPA, data-center rent or debt-service obligations. If a customer defaults, contractual rights still require enforcement before becoming cash. Power or wafer capacity therefore cannot be called risk-free "land rights."

ASML's Q3 revenue and operating cash performance disclosed on October 14, 2026 was strong, providing evidence that the supply chain is realizing gains, contrary to the pessimistic case. But its cash flow includes working-capital changes; one quarter's cash cannot all be annualized, and an equipment supplier earning money in the current quarter does not establish that all customer projects will pay back. ASML Q3 2026 US GAAP financial statements, income statement and cash flow statement

"Capital providers holding liquidity" can wait for conditions to improve, but waiting itself has an opportunity cost. Corporate operating cash, fund capital subject to investment mandates, government budgets and sovereign-fund commitments cannot all be treated as a pool of cash ready to enter AI projects at any time, still less used to name the ultimate winners.

VII. Testing the Opposing Case Must Allow More Than a Later Bubble Collapse

This framework must allow five developments sufficient to change the original judgment: end-customer payments materialize faster than infrastructure; falling costs for equivalent-quality tasks expand demand and improve cash gross profit; supplier investments help customers through construction, after which customers no longer depend on injections; policy or long-term capital improves the maturity structure without continuing to expand low-return expenditure; and greater technological capability turns previously unbillable tasks into stable businesses. The first four do not require an announcement that AGI has arrived. The fifth also needs evidence from tasks, production deployment and paid retention; a single benchmark score cannot make the case.

These forms of disconfirming evidence affect revenue, cost, funding sources, maturities or asset uses respectively, and cannot substitute for one another. Rising share prices and successful financing mainly show that markets remain willing to supply capital; improving customer cash gross profit and renewals are closer to project economics. If disconfirming evidence persists, the corresponding capital-recovery risk assessment should be reduced, rather than explaining every case as "the longer it is propped up, the more dangerous the end."

Historical analogies offer no transferable uniform similarity score, amplification factor or number of years to clear the market. Whether AI projects ultimately recover their capital must be assessed through their own billable use, cost curves, delivery progress, financing terms and competitive landscape. The questions supplied by history still require answers in these companies' cash flows.

Appendix D: Alphabet's Financing Case - From Planned Capacity to Per-Share Value

Information cutoff: 2026-10-31 (Beijing time). All amounts are in USD billions; share prices and earnings per share are in USD. The Q3 earnings release and September 30 10-Q have been published; the interim financial statements are unaudited. ATM issuance, bond financing and cash balances have been rolled forward from the Q2 baseline to Q3. Announced plans, completed issuance, actual cash and future optional capacity remain separately presented.

D.1 The USD 80 Billion Plan Exists, but It Is Not USD 80 Billion Received at Once

This round of Alphabet financing has moved from planning into further execution: the initial 6/1 plan had an 80 ceiling; common and preferred stock financing generated net cash of 49.562 in June; Q3 brought a further 12.371 in net cash through the ATM program; and net cash from the two types of equity financing totaled 61.933 for the first nine months. Approximately 27.6 of ATM capacity remained as of 9/30. New shares, preferred-stock rights and debt interest have all entered existing shareholders' cash and per-share models. The current position can no longer be described as "the ATM has not yet been used."

The structure in the formal 6/1 filing comprised an underwritten offering of 30, an ATM program of 40 and a private placement of 10 to Berkshire affiliates; the underwritten portion included common stock and mandatory convertible preferred stock. This document establishes the financing plan at that time, not full execution, and still less that management determined operating cash would be insufficient for the next three years. Initial 6/1 financing filing

Document or event date What happened Treatment in reconciliation

Common stock completed on 6/4 Public offering of 29 million shares each of Class A and Class C; Berkshire private placement of 14 million shares of each class Public-offering net proceeds approximately 20.5, private placement 10; total newly issued common shares of 86 million
Preferred stock completed on 6/5 6.25% mandatory convertible preferred stock Notes disclose net proceeds of approximately 19.0; future conversion and dividends modeled separately

Sources: Alphabet Q2 10-Q, cash flow statement, Note 11 and liquidity section, ATM supplement, Final 8/6 bond prospectus.

The difference between 49.562 in the cash flow statement and 49.5 from summing rounded note components reflects presentation precision, not an omitted piece of "mysterious financing." Common stock, preferred stock, employee shares and the statement of changes in shareholders' equity also have distinct accounting scopes. This analysis therefore uses the cash flow statement for cash and issuance notes for share counts, without mixing them into a custom precise figure.

This reconciliation now extends through September 30. Q3 ATM components of 4.3 and 8.1 are rounded disclosure figures; their sum cannot replace the cash flow statement's 12.371. Similarly, "net senior notes of 28.7" and "total net debt issuance of 28.996" have different scopes. USD note issuance included 23.8 at fixed rates and 1.3 at floating rates, alongside two AUD tranches, so the original August plan of 25 cannot be called the whole quarter's total financing. The execution above is reconciled between the Q3 10-Q and the accompanying release, but both remain disclosures from the same company and are not presented as two independent sources. Alphabet Q3 10-Q, debt, equity and cash flows, Q3 release, pages 2 and 6

D.2 Ask "What Is the New Money For?" and Check "What Is the Existing Money?"

As of 9/30, Alphabet had cash and cash equivalents of 56.334 and marketable securities of 208.764, totaling 265.098. The securities included equity investments of 90.384, of which 75.2 was SpaceX stock subject to short-term sale restrictions. Cash, government and debt securities, and restricted equity are not the same form of liquidity. The figure 265.098 can describe the reported combined assets, but cannot all be called cash immediately available to pay construction bills. The carrying value of long-term debt of 125.102 at the same date also differs from note principal and includes the effects of discounts, hedges and current-portion classification. Q3 10-Q, balance sheet, investment and debt notes

The operating cash-timing pressure has continued beyond a single negative quarter: Q3 operating cash of 42.290 and cash capital expenditures of 51.355 produced FCF of −9.065, more negative than Q2's −5.855; trailing-twelve-month FCF remained +19.747. Cloud operating profit rose to 11.121 over the same period, showing that operating demand and group-level asset-payment pressure can rise simultaneously. Cloud profit cannot prove that every new batch of assets has paid back, nor can a negative group quarter be permanently extrapolated into an operating breakdown. Official Q3 financial results and FCF reconciliation

Uses of proceeds should continue to be recorded separately according to the documents. Completed common- and preferred-stock proceeds are for general corporate purposes, including AI infrastructure; the ATM program is primarily for tax obligations related to employee equity awards. Q3 ATM execution does not change this distinction. Neither the 12.371 received nor the remaining 27.6 of capacity can therefore be allocated entirely to new AI data centers, and the entire group's cash shortfall cannot be attributed to AI.

Financing may simultaneously support construction, cash management, acquisitions and capital-structure choices. Only by aligning these uses with cash balances, debt maturities, operating expenditure and shareholder returns can one judge whether financing proactively adds headroom or fills a gap for payments that cannot be postponed. Issuing shares alone does not yield a unique answer.

D.3 For Existing Shareholders, the Real Calculation Is Per-Share Value

Financing expands company assets but also adds claims for new shareholders or holders of preferred rights. Looking only at the amount raised or the increase in shares shows just one side. The test is whether the future cash purchased with those funds can exceed the additional claims and financing costs.

D.3.1 Common Stock: More Shares Do Not Automatically Mean Value Destruction

In a simplified model excluding preferred stock, let pre-financing common-equity value be V₀, the share count N₀, net issuance cash C, newly issued shares ΔN, and the net present value created by new projects after deducting investment NPV. Then:

Post-financing value per share =（V₀ + C + NPV）/（N₀ + ΔN）.

If the proceeds remain on the balance sheet, first include them as cash; if they are invested in projects, project NPV must already deduct the investment, so that cash and the same project asset are not counted twice. Issuance expenses, taxes and control-right differences should also be included. Do not simply divide the financing amount by total market capitalization to claim a permanent percentage loss.

A purely illustrative example: initial value is 1000 and the share count 10, giving pre-financing value of 100 per share. Issuing 0.1 new shares at 100 each raises cash of 10. If project NPV is zero, post-financing value of 1010/10.1 remains 100 per share. Although each share owns a smaller proportion of the company, no value is lost under this no-cost assumption. If new-project NPV is −5, value per share is approximately 99.50; if it is +5, approximately 100.50. This is not an Alphabet price target; it only illustrates why financing price and project returns must be calculated together.

The June public common-stock offering and private placement added 86 million shares, and the Q3 ATM issued another 36 million. Their combined 122 million shares can be used as the issuance volume for these specific financing transactions. It cannot be added again to the net increase in common shares for the entire period, which also includes employee shares, repurchases and other items. Period-end common shares at 9/30 were 12.294 billion, while Q3 diluted weighted-average shares were 12.368 billion. They measure different populations and time weights; the quarter's EPS cannot be reverse-engineered directly from the increase in period-end shares. Q3 10-Q, equity and EPS notes

D.3.2 Mandatory Convertible Preferred Stock: Check Dividends and Conversion Terms First

The preferred stock carries a 6.25% dividend, payable in cash, common stock or a combination at the company's option. Mandatory conversion normally occurs in May 2029, with the share count depending on the price range of the relevant share class and anti-dilution provisions. The company also paid approximately 1.0 for a capped call to reduce some conversion dilution. This is not cost-free funding, nor can it be broadly described as "no current dilution and no additional burden ever." Q2 10-Q, Note 11 and earnings-per-share note

Q3 recognized preferred-stock dividends of 0.298; subtracting these from net income of 26.468 left income attributable to common stock of 26.170. This shows that preferred rights already affect the current common-share earnings measure; one cannot focus only on the share count at future conversion. This is also a separate item from non-operating gains and losses caused by fair-value changes in the company's equity securities, and each should enter the per-share bridge separately. Q3 10-Q, earnings-per-share note

The research model should separately list current preferred dividends, conversion share counts, the capped call's effective range and cost, and follow the EPS note's anti-dilution treatment. Without a tranche-by-tranche model, no reliable range for permanent dilution or future EPS changes can be provided.

Financing instrument Rights and cash to list first Position in the existing-shareholder model
Common stock Net cash proceeds, new shares, issue price and expenses Cash or project value enters the numerator; additional shares enter the denominator
Mandatory convertible preferred stock Cumulative dividends, conversion date, price range, anti-dilution and capped call Periodic dividends and future conversion shares, with EPS rules checked separately
ATM capacity Actual issuance dates, shares, net proceeds and remaining capacity No cash received recorded before execution; no assumption of all dilution at once
Fixed-rate bonds Net proceeds, coupons, maturities and covenants Interest, debt-repayment cash and financing repricing; no direct addition to common shares
Floating-rate loans Reference rate, spread, repricing date and balance Rate changes flow through on contractual dates; policy rates are not applied directly

Bond financing adds interest and principal due at maturity, but does not directly increase common shares. Common stock, preferred stock and bonds cannot be placed in a universally valid ranking "from cheapest to most expensive": financing timing, issue price, tax shields, rating constraints and investment returns all change the actual cost.

D.4 Evidence Boundaries for External Investor Participation

This review reread Note 11 of the Q2 10-Q and confirmed Berkshire affiliates' participation in the private placement of 10. It is an external funding source. Investing in Alphabet does not turn it into an internal loop between cloud providers and model companies, still less an "endorsement at an above-fair-value price" without a pricing comparison.

This investment establishes only that an institution accepted the relevant price and rights. It does not prove optimism about all AI assets, nor replace a calculation of Alphabet's project returns. Who participated in the decision, why this company was selected, and whether other companies were reduced at the same time each require their own public statements or holdings evidence. Inferring from a single private placement that a particular individual has concluded other giants will collapse is not a verifiable conclusion.

Further research on Berkshire should obtain quarterly 13Fs, check share classes, share counts and reporting dates, and separate purchases from changes in the market value of holdings. Quarter-end disclosures still do not equal real-time positions on October 31, and actual investment cannot be inferred from differences in market value. This appendix does not incorporate a holdings trajectory whose verification remains incomplete.

D.5 Historical Comparisons Should Compare Rights, Not Guess the Top

Historical financing comparisons must first establish whether the instruments are alike: common stock's residual claim, preferred dividends and conversion terms, upside participation through attached warrants, and debt's repayment at maturity carry different risks. Valuations at financing, counterparty liquidity and investors' bargaining positions also need to be compared together. Preferred rights do not equal risk-free protection, and participation through common stock does not mean an absence of investment value.

Studying the timing ability of financing decisions requires checking original issuance documents transaction by transaction, including issuance cases that were not followed by a crash, and harmonizing event windows, prices or total returns, market benchmarks and sample selection. Searching backward for financing only among companies that subsequently collapsed creates selection bias; it does not establish that Alphabet will follow the same path after financing.

The lessons worth retaining are these: financing addresses the ability to pay but does not automatically solve investment returns; participation by a powerful investor does not eliminate subsequent capital losses; and equity financing may improve debt-paying capacity, making construction more resilient to volatility. Whether it benefits or dilutes existing shareholders must be decided jointly by the issue price, use of proceeds and cash recovery.

The October 28 FOMC decision held the range at 3.75%-4.00%, with two dissenters favoring a 25-basis-point increase; this supersedes the previous edition's "next decision pending" status. Outstanding fixed coupons, floating-rate repricing, future financing and the equity discount rate work through different mechanisms. The policy range cannot be added directly to every Alphabet debt instrument, nor does an unchanged rate mean financing costs have stopped changing. Official FOMC October 28 decision

D.6 How the Next Financial Report Would Change This Appendix's Conclusion

Subsequent evidence Where to update Inference to avoid

This appendix concludes that financing has progressed further into execution: ATM issuance increases common shares, preferred stock generates current dividends, and multicurrency bonds broaden debt funding sources. At the same time, Cloud profit is growing while the group's construction cash shortfall is widening. The initial 80 cannot all be treated as received, but financing can no longer be described merely as a plan. Whether existing shareholders benefit must be decided jointly by issue price, use of proceeds, project net cash and per-share rights. This appendix does not infer a crash date or individual-stock drawdown range from financing size.

Appendix E: Fact Checks and Evidence Boundaries

This table supports the current V1.9 judgments, with information through 31 October 2026, Beijing time; interest rates use US observations for 30 October. F01-F16 carry forward the verified V1.8 baseline; this review deepens video-model, microdrama-duration, and coding-labor-cost research. The body distinguishes disclosed facts, company forward-looking statements, third-party estimates, the author's conditional illustrations, and unverified matters. Company IR materials, SEC statements, and republications can cross-check excerpts, but one disclosing entity does not create several independent sets of operating evidence. Cross-review tests reasoning; it does not replace auditors or evidence of end-customer cash.

E.1 Key Facts Currently Used and Areas Deepened in This Review

F01 | Microsoft: Quarters, Segments, and Leases. FY27Q1 results announced on 28 October reported revenue of USD 92.279B and operating profit of 45.915B for the period ended 30 September. Operating cash of 58.000B less cash capital purchases of 39.367B gives 18.633B; deducting another 1.000B of finance-lease principal gives 17.633B. The latter is this report's explicitly bounded cash bridge, not a newly defined uniform company FCFF. Earnings-call CapEx, new finance leases, and 10-Q additions to right-of-use assets differ in scope and cannot be forced to reconcile or deducted twice. Company-restated figures are used for the new fiscal year's segment reorganization and Azure definition changes; old “Azure and other cloud services” figures cannot simply be spliced in. Original results. Lease notes and restatement details are in the earnings chapter; differences remain where complete bridges were unavailable.

At 14:00 US daylight time on 28 October (02:00 Beijing time on 29 October), the committee voted 10-2 to hold 3.75%-4.00%; the two dissenters favored a 25-basis-point increase. This was not a rate cut and cannot be described as an additional increase already implemented. Federal Reserve official statement.

F20 | Upstream Video Price-Volume Model: Scenario Scale Is Not a Market Forecast. The 120 minutes, 80% AI share, 25% reuse, 720 advertising seconds, 960 localization seconds, 25% acceptance yield, and 90% billed share in 4.12 are all assumptions; the public H3 output price supplies only a price reference. Newly accepted volume is 6000 seconds, successful output 24000 seconds, billed output 21600 seconds, and narrowly defined output revenue USD 1728. Retries are already in the acceptance-yield denominator and cannot be multiplied again; free volume still incurs inference costs. With prices halved and acceptance yield doubled in 4.19, volume k corresponds to revenue k/4 and assumed contribution 0.1875k, requiring k of 4 and approximately 5.33 respectively to break even. Compute efficiency, cost changes, and incremental demand are not described as measured facts. Internal group calls are not consolidated external revenue; repeat viewing does not mean repeated generation.

F21 | Coding Organizational Evidence: Do Not Attach New Coefficients to Old Samples. Chen-Stratton uses the 28 October revision with 655 companies, 720542 workers cumulatively observed, and approximately 445 million events, not the old 718-company sample. Agent coefficients are incremental to earlier assistants; lines of code, issues, epics, and employment have distinct definitions and statistical intervals. No significant detected employment effect does not mean zero. NBER's cumulative commit effects for completion, interactive agents, and autonomous agents are not summed to 360%. The 4867-person RCT's +26.08% task-output effect implies approximately 20.685% time savings for equal output only under very strong comparability assumptions, not 26.08% wage savings. METR's old-model negative findings and subsequent selection bias are both retained. Chen-Stratton, NBER original, RCT

F22 | Labor and Wages: Do Not Rename Descriptive Gaps as Causal Layoff Rates. Stanford's 19% relative employment-path gap for young workers in highly exposed occupations is concentrated in hiring, includes multiple occupations, and is not a causal estimate. The Fed employment-trend gap, Epoch task-transfer survey, and announced company cuts retain their own definitions and do not yield a Chinese displacement rate. US BLS occupational medians, Chinese National Bureau of Statistics industry averages, and professional/technical-personnel means cannot be directly compared or added into a global wage pool; employer benefits cannot use one universal percentage either. Original periods, samples, and definitions remain in 5.9-5.10. Stanford, BLS, National Bureau of Statistics

F23 | Labor ROI: Keep Capacity, Cash, and Risk Budgets Separate. The US 10-person and Chinese 8-person teams are author assumptions, including adoption, gross time savings, and incremental review, rework, and governance. Steady-state net hours released are 2400/900, with net capacity values of USD 200000/RMB 30000. Only explicit conditions for avoidable hiring, outsourcing, or similar expenditure produce the alternative cash paths of USD 140000/RMB 108000; these cannot be added. First-year learning and integration are deducted separately, and risk allowances are not described as actual incident payments. Fixed-budget and fixed-headcount formulas illustrate capacity without bottlenecks and with continuously adjustable resources. Demand elasticity is uncalibrated; no industry job-decline rate follows. Complete inputs, sensitivities, and formula-by-formula recalculations are in 5.11-5.14 and the calculation script.

E.3 Claims Excluded from Current Conclusions

  • Industry-wide AI returns without independent evidence of end-customer payments, or undeduplicated totals of circular financing, procurement, and guarantees.
  • Inferring realized revenue from downloads, plays, popularity, or surveyed willingness to pay; inferring an AI-generation market from all microdramas; calling global or ex-China data US data.
  • Directly translating lower production cost into higher margins while omitting acquisition, refunds, failed productions, labor, and rights costs; replacing industry averages with platform-reported hits.
  • Presenting company guidance, third-party forecasts, annualized run rates, or scenario illustrations as realized financial results; concealing single-source access restrictions as “multiple-party verification.”
  • Declaring insolvency or collapsing demand directly from negative FCF; proving capital returns from completed financing, an IPO filing, or a large contract.
  • Generating new target prices, default probabilities, and crash dates from old prices, currently unverified CDS, or arbitrary scenario weights.
  • Retrofitting old conditional seat illustrations into forecasts with specific target years, or rewriting the original draft's longstanding acknowledgment of charges across fields as “we once forecast that only coding could earn money.”
  • Generalizing subproduct ARR, group revenue, user activity, or court-restructuring status to an entire industry; conflating API promotions, restricted previews, open weights, and unconditional commercial licenses.
  • Renaming mobile-app usage per user as total film viewing across all devices; calculating video-model generation demand from repeat viewing.
  • Treating work output, jobs, wages, resource value, and avoidable cash as one metric; upgrading observational employment gaps into AI-caused layoff proportions.

Appendix F: What Fusion Energy Can Change, and What It Cannot Replace

In this round of review, the official Helion Orion and CFS SPARC pages were reread on 31 October 2026 (Beijing time): Orion still lists initial operations in 2028 and a design capacity of at least 50MW as targets, while SPARC still lists Q>1 in 2027 as a target. Neither can be reclassified as having achieved commercial electricity supply. The original disclosure and observation dates are retained for the other projects; no site-by-site acceptance review was conducted. New power sources can change construction constraints and cost curves, but whether they improve recovery of AI capital still has to pass four tests: commercial electricity supply, system cost, competitive pass-through and customer payment.

F.1 First Separate the Three Clocks

The first is the time for an experimental device to reach its physical target; the second is the time for a power plant to deliver net electricity continuously to the grid; the third is the time for electricity to reach data centers where it is needed, at a competitive price. A faster first clock warrants greater attention to the other two, but does not allow future cheap electricity to be entered directly into this year's inference costs.

Demonstration devices, first-of-a-kind power plants and large-scale replication must each have separate validation conditions. Projects may be delayed or brought forward, but without engineering progress, contractual acceptance terms and an independent probability model, one cannot predict that a particular power purchase agreement will certainly be fulfilled or is highly likely to default. A construction announcement is not commercial operation either.

F.2 Disclosed Milestones for Two Approaches

Approach Company disclosures and observation boundaries Items that cannot yet be treated as completed
Helion Orion The company's project page lists site construction in 2025 and generator-building construction in 2026; initial operations are targeted for 2028, with a facility design supply capacity of at least 50MW to fulfill the Microsoft power purchase agreement; initial operations are not equated with acceptance of full supply capacity Continuous net generation, contractual electricity-supply acceptance, availability, operating and maintenance costs, and the pace of subsequent replication
CFS SPARC The official technology page still lists Q>1 in 2027 as a target; this is a device intended to validate technology for a subsequent power plant Q>1 does not mean an existing commercial power plant is selling electricity to the grid, or that all engineering and economic hurdles have been cleared
CFS ARC The first power plant targets approximately 400MW of grid supply in the early 2030s; papers on its physics basis were published in June 2026, and progress on design and engineering milestones was reported in August Physics-design papers and milestone reviews do not mean that 400MW has been delivered; final capital cost and the economics of electricity sales remain to be realized

Sources: Helion Orion project page, CFS SPARC technology explanation, ARC project description, June 2026 physics-basis paper announcement, August 2026 engineering progress. The targets come from the project developers; they are not independent forecasts of success probabilities.

The system boundary must also be stated when measuring Q: the ratio of energy released by fusion to plasma-heating input is not the ratio of electricity output from the entire plant to all electricity consumed. Even if physical gain reaches its target, auxiliary systems, conversion efficiency, maintenance outages and material lifetimes still affect the electricity available for sale. These different measures cannot all be abbreviated as "net generation validated."

Another, nearer-term engineering milestone was NVIDIA's launch of the DSX Ready program on September 21, initially covering BESS (battery energy storage systems) and CDU (coolant distribution units), with Vertiv and others participating in the liquid-cooling equipment qualification process. It helps check whether components meet requirements, but cannot replace site engineering design and is not evidence of commercial electricity supply from a fusion plant. Component qualification, whole-site acceptance and actual customer payment should be kept separate. NVIDIA DSX Ready explanation

On September 28, Crusoe confirmed that it is the developer of Google's campus in Armstrong County, Texas. Construction began in June 2025 and is still underway; it cannot be recorded as a new start this week. The power plan includes direct connections to two wind farms of 265.5MW each, one operating and the other under construction. Rated wind capacity of 531MW is neither energized data-center IT load nor continuously available power. The announcement did not disclose the project's investment amount or cash payback period; it is not added to the table of realized capital expenditures or treated as a breakthrough in fusion. Crusoe project announcement

On October 2, Oracle announced plans to subscribe to part of the power purchase arrangements for the existing Point Beach nuclear plant, expecting to absorb approximately USD 300 million in energy costs for local residents; approval by the Public Service Commission of Wisconsin is still required. This reminds researchers to include energy and local infrastructure responsibilities in data-center costs, but does not represent payment already made, approved electricity supply, or new nuclear generation capacity. Specific duration and annual payment schedules were not disclosed, so USD 300 million is not deducted directly from this quarter's free cash flow, and this nuclear-fission arrangement is certainly not treated as fusion implementation. Oracle Point Beach announcement

F.3 How Cheap Electricity Enters a Cost Statement

The cost of inference services includes not only electricity but also depreciation of accelerators and data-center facilities, networking and storage, personnel, software, spare parts, financing and other operations and maintenance. Cost structures, equipment utilization and accounting classifications vary across companies, and there is no uniform electricity-cost share for training, inference or different data centers. A model should use the target facility's electricity expenditure, effective consumption and total costs as its calculation bases, rather than replacing project data with an industry percentage.

A more reliable approach is an explicitly hypothetical calculation. Assume a service has revenue of 100 and a cost of service of 60, including electricity costs of 12. Electricity then accounts for 20% of service costs, and the original gross margin is 40%. If the electricity price falls by half and all other variables are unchanged, cost falls from 60 to 54 and gross margin rises to 46%, an improvement of 6 percentage points. In the extreme case of electricity costs falling to zero, gross margin becomes 52%; one must not add "electricity is 20% of costs" directly to the 40% gross margin to obtain 60%.

Electricity price relative to baseline Electricity cost Total cost of service Gross margin
100% 12 60 40%
75% 9 57 43%
50% 6 54 46%
0% (extreme sensitivity case) 0 48 52%

All figures in the table are teaching assumptions; they represent neither electricity prices for any fusion project nor actual cloud-provider gross margins. A real model must also include the costs of connecting a new power source, transmission and distribution, backup power and retrofits. It cannot simply lower the electricity price while omitting the capital investment required to obtain that price.

The cost statement can also be taken one step further into cash flow: if revenue of 100 and original electricity costs of 12 are both annual figures, halving the electricity price saves 6 annually, but obtaining the low-cost source requires an additional investment of 10 at the outset. Temporarily excluding taxes, financing, maintenance differences and residual value, and assuming the savings arrive at each year-end for five consecutive years, discounted at 10%, the incremental project's net present value is approximately 12.74. If competitive price cuts pass all the savings to customers, the incremental cash retained by the service provider becomes zero, and the additional investment has a value of −10 under these assumptions. Both numbers are merely examples with clear boundaries; they are not used to value fusion-power projects before actual electricity prices, operating life and customer pricing have been validated.

At project level, the levelized cost of electricity also needs to be calculated: using the same discount rate and lifetime boundaries, divide the present value of construction, operation, fuel, maintenance, decommissioning and other costs by the present value of deliverable net electricity. This denominator must deduct internal consumption and outage effects; the instantaneous power of a reactor cannot substitute for annual electricity sales. Transmission and distribution, backup and data-center retrofit costs beyond the grid connection point must then be added to the customer's delivered cost. Even if a particular approach has cheap fuel, that does not mean the electricity customer's full bill approaches zero.

F.4 Who Receives the Profit Matters as Much as Lower Costs

If cheap electricity is available in only a few locations, owners controlling power sources and grid-connection resources may retain some of the gains. If competitors can all obtain the same electricity price, cloud-service and API price cuts may pass the savings to customers. Industry output may therefore grow without supplier margins necessarily rising alongside it.

In the illustration above, if the price also falls from 100 to 94 and cost is 54, gross profit remains 40 and gross margin is approximately 42.6%. Customers obtain a lower price, while the service provider does not receive the full profit improvement in the earlier scenario. This does not mean price cuts must harm service providers: if usage, customer retention and the spreading of fixed costs improve, total profit may still increase. Volume, price and unit cost must be calculated together.

Fusion power may therefore relieve physical bottlenecks, but may also drive additional supply and another round of price competition. Its effect on AI valuations depends on whether the gains ultimately accrue to generators, cloud providers, application companies or end customers; it cannot uniformly be labeled positive or negative.

F.5 An Energy Breakthrough Does Not Mean AGI Has Been Achieved

More and cheaper electricity can expand affordable training and inference budgets, but does not directly establish that models can consistently handle long tasks, recognize errors, respect permissions or act reliably in unfamiliar environments. Capability, reliability and economics are related but distinct variables, and should be validated separately.

Conversely, this also provides insufficient evidence to conclude that algorithms have hit a fixed ceiling or that usable data has been exhausted. Algorithmic improvements, better data quality, inference-time compute and task decomposition can all change how much electricity is needed to obtain a given capability. If computing efficiency improves substantially, additional electricity remains valuable, but its scarcity and the allocation of rents may change.

F.6 Which Links Fusion and Equity Financing Each Change

If commercialized, fusion primarily changes available electricity, construction locations and service costs. Equity financing primarily changes the source of cash, debt pressure and the allocation of equity per share. Both may extend the time over which a project can be executed, but neither alone proves that its net present value is positive.

Equity financing is not sufficient evidence of "failure to earn a return on capital." If proceeds from new shares are invested in high-return projects, existing shareholders may gain per-share value despite dilution. If new funds merely sustain expansion without end-customer cash collection, an improved cash balance and value creation diverge. For Alphabet's actual financing status, see Section 2.8 of the circular-investment chapter. Planned capacity, securities issued and net proceeds received are checked separately; the full financial statements are not repeated here.

F.7 Why the Year of the Next Bubble Cannot Be Fixed in Advance

A new technology may attract narratives and capital, but that is still insufficient to conclude that "2028-2029 will inevitably be the turn of the fusion bubble." Research should observe whether financing valuations, engineering milestones, customer payments, generation costs and available exit routes diverge, rather than impose a calendar on capital rotation.

Comparing fusion-company valuations requires current original financing documents and capital structures; IPO dates require formal filings and issuance arrangements, not technological milestones as substitutes. Even a genuine valuation cannot replace technological readiness, and even genuine engineering progress cannot guarantee public-market liquidity.

In terms of research coverage, Helion and CFS have the project materials listed in this chapter. TAE Technologies, Tokamak Energy and Pacific Fusion remain candidate approaches followed in the original report; company valuations, commissioning dates and IPO years that have not been rechecked for the current period are not carried forward. Before upgrading them to formal company research, obtain equipment configurations, independent measurements, engineering costs and customer contracts, rather than wait for a preset year of the "next bubble."

Research subject Information usable in this chapter Decisive evidence still missing
Helion / Orion Construction status, initial-operations target and design supply capacity on the company project page Actual continuous net electricity output, commercial acceptance, cost and availability
CFS / SPARC, ARC The experimental-gain target and first-power-plant plans disclosed separately by the company Q boundaries, whole-plant net electricity, construction schedule, capital cost and electricity-sale terms
TAE, Tokamak Energy, Pacific Fusion Retained as candidate approaches for verification; no current valuations or commissioning years entered Original engineering materials, completed milestones and independent verification

F.8 Evidence Ledger for Follow-Up

Evidence to record Why it affects the judgment Materials that cannot replace it
Verifiable continuous net electricity output, operating duration and outage records Distinguishes a one-off physical achievement from an available power source News headlines, rendered model images
Whole-plant capital cost, financing cost, maintenance and material-replacement costs Enables calculation of levelized electricity cost and stress scenarios Fuel cost alone or experimental energy gain
Grid-connection progress and actual deliverable location Determines whether a specific data center can be served Total national generation potential
Customer-contract prices, duration, minimum purchases and acceptance conditions Shows who bears technology and delivery risk Maximum MW announced in a contract
Changes in cloud-service prices, usage and gross profit Determines how savings are allocated A single electricity-price reduction percentage
Cash payback period for new AI assets Tests whether power improvements translate into shareholder returns The company's overall share price or new financing amount

F.9 What Would Change the Judgment

If a project completes commercial power-supply, reliability and cost acceptance early, the available-power scenario should be brought forward and the benefiting data centers recalculated, rather than adhering to the original timetable. If electricity has been delivered but connection and backup costs offset most savings, the nominal reduction in generation cost cannot be copied directly into cloud-service costs.

If cheap electricity significantly reduces the cost per successful task, expands end-customer payments, and generates incremental cash flow sufficient to cover construction and maintenance investment, the assessment of improved commercial capital recovery should be raised. If capacity expands quickly, price pass-through is even faster, and profits do not remain with suppliers, the distribution of industry-chain gains should be reassessed, rather than broadly marking down the value of the entire AI industry.

This chapter's conclusion is conditional: energy innovation can change the constraints on the AI industry, and capital markets can change the financing conditions for construction. Only when these changes ultimately enter sustainable cash flow do they change asset value. One cannot preemptively reject new evidence on the grounds that "the bubble must burst," or skip cash recovery on the grounds that "fusion is about to succeed."

Research Closing: Let Subsequent Facts Test the Judgment

Whether AI can transform production and everyday life, whether AI infrastructure can earn back its cost of capital, and whether a security is worth owning at a particular price are three related questions that cannot substitute for one another. Substantive technological progress will not make every investment pay off; excessive capital-market pricing will not erase technological capabilities that have already been developed. Separating these three questions is the only way to preserve room for discussion between optimism about the industry and financial caution.

The most important work in this research is not to find a sufficiently eye-catching score, but to trace a unit of capital from financing, procurement, construction and delivery through customer use, revenue recognition, cash collection and debt repayment. Each stage in the chain has its own dates and evidence: an announced contract is not money received; pledged equipment is not cash that can be realized at any time; revenue growth is not free-cash-flow growth; and a falling share price does not mean the business has already failed. Only by checking each item can we determine whether risk remains a matter of valuation or is entering the company's ability to meet its payments.

Research of this kind will not produce an answer that never changes. If paid demand expands, customer collections improve, and unit-cost reductions are not entirely passed on through price competition, existing capacity may be absorbed over time, and the previously cautious judgment must weaken accordingly. If long-term payment commitments continue to grow while actual use, profits and collections fail to keep pace, risk may merely have moved elsewhere, even if financing remains readily available. Contradictory facts are not the enemy of a conclusion; they are a necessary condition for keeping a judgment valid.

The role of historical analogies also ends there. Shale oil, the internet and other capital cycles can draw attention to financing maturities, the inertia of capacity expansion and the distribution of technological gains, but they cannot assign bankruptcy dates to today's companies, still less guarantee returns to today's investors. Today's business models, cash reserves, policy environment and market structure all require fresh examination. Similarities supply the questions; differences determine the answers.

This report therefore retains its unresolved questions: some contractual terms remain undisclosed; gaps remain between usable project capacity and actual billable utilization; there is no uniform formula for allocating social productivity gains and industry-chain profits; and financial institutions' ultimate look-through risk exposure is still not fully visible. These gaps should neither be concealed by a precise probability nor filled with promotional certainty. They should become the starting point for the next round of evidence gathering.

What readers should ultimately take away is a method of judgment that can be reviewed: knowing what they believe, which document, calculation and assumption that belief depends on, and what evidence should make them change their minds. The market will not prove a view on a research report's timetable. Research can, however, hold itself to a stricter standard of responsibility: do not present the unknown as known, a scenario as a prophecy, or analysis as action beyond the authority granted.
